#6978. ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Planning Huge Rule-Change Next Season to Stop These Celebs From Being Voted Off

Dancing With the Stars has officially wrapped season 35, and the show is already preparing for season 36 next fall. And apparently producers are making a huge change to the voting process to prevent high-scoring couples (think: Jenna Dewan and Val Chmerkovskiy) from being eliminated too early in the competition.

“Producers have already discussed bringing back the judges’ save for next season. Again, it’s still early but that is more than likely going to be brought back,” a source tells The Sun. “They’ve already made it clear they want to shake it up next season, drastically.”

The judges’ save (which was previously in effect from Season 28 to Season 32) lets the judges step in and decide which of the bottom two pairings goes home after fans have already voted. Ultimately, it’s a way to counter fan favoritism overriding high scores.

DWTS abandoned the judges’ save because it ultimately gave the experts more power than viewers at home. As executive producer Deena Katz told TV Line, “I think it should be about America. And at the end of the day, the judges' save sometimes isn't what America wanted. And [eliminations are based on] half the judges and half America voting, and so that's kind of the reason we did it.”

FYI, Dancing With the Stars’ UK version, Strictly Come Dancing, still uses the judges’ save, and judge Shirley Ballas explained to Extra, “I think it's good that the audience choose. They put two in the bottom. That was their choice. And then, at the end of the day, we get to save the dancer that we think is the best. Because sometimes, on your kind of show... they could send all the good dancers home. And then, you know, you're left with a different final. So it's important that there's something for everybody.”

#6974. Getting Ready With Jared McCain

The chronically online NBA player’s routine is all about preserving his mental health. And yes, he has read your comments about his nails.

Jared McCain loves a routine—several routines, actually, which is why there’s a whiteboard in his bathroom, covered with a bulleted list of his daily to-dos (journaling, affirmations, bone broth, and lymphatic jumps, among them). That’s all separate from the 22-year-old’s bedtime and wake-up regimens (silk eye mask, silk pillowcase, silk bonnet, magnesium, and mouth tape, then a multistep skincare process, respectively). And this doesn’t even include many of the things his 7.7 million social media followers have come to love: Jared hanging out in de-puffing eye patches. Jared wandering around wearing Gen Z’s favorite pimple patches. Jared flashing his latest bright-colored manicure.

As he moves through his Oklahoma City home, where he lives with three of his best friends, it’s clear that none of the above is purely performative, as is so often the case with internet celebrities. “I do a lot of skincare because I sweat a lot,” he says as he works his way through what looks like the entire Hailey Bieber–founded Rhode skincare line. “I really like to take care of my face.”

Click through the videos on his TikTok or Instagram page and you’ll likely see that face illuminated by LED lights. “One of my favorite parts about my house is my red light showerhead,” he says. “Red light just creates this vibe and gives me health benefits. It’s number one in my life.” It’s also on his whiteboard three times.

Jared first broke into virality as a starting guard on Duke University’s basketball team. And while his talent on the court would lead him to the NBA—he now plays for the Oklahoma City Thunder—it was something else that captured the wider world’s attention. “I started painting my nails during COVID, and that’s when I also started on TikTok,” he explains. “I played with the polish on and I had a really good game, so I thought, Hold on, this might be the new thing.” (He now has brand partnerships with Sally Hansen and Ulta Beauty.)

“I didn’t really care what other people thought about it,” he says of any negative feedback he got at the time. He now keeps manicure inspiration cataloged on a Pinterest board—“most of the time, I’m feeling colorful; I love bright, bright colors”—and his Oklahoma City–based nail artist Yvie McCrary often sends him ideas to prep for his next appointment.

“In the NBA, there can be a lot of scrutiny, so I try to take care of my mental health,” he adds. In his downstairs “energy room,” he flicks on an LED panel (of course) for his daily yoga flow. “I love finding peace,” he says. “Meditation allows me to assess what’s going on in my life.” It’s yet another habit he uses to work through any unfavorable comments or haters he comes across: “It’s funny at this point. I’ve gotten used to it by now. It’s a blessing to even be talked about.”

Not that everything Jared does is 100 percent zen. When he’s not indulging in self-betterment practices, he’s posting TikTok dances and lip syncs from the Thunder’s locker room, sometimes with special appearances from his teammates.

“Entertaining has always been so fun to me,” he says. “That’s kind of what basketball is, too. Growing up, I thought I was going to be on the Disney Channel or a pop star. I always end up singing some Olivia Dean.” His plea for the singer to bring him onstage during her Art of Loving Tour stop at Madison Square Garden in New York City got nearly 2 million views (sadly, she did not invite him up onstage).

He launches into a rendition of Olivia’s “A Couple Minutes” with surprisingly impressive vocals. “The song could make me cry right now,” he confesses. One of his roommates later jokes, “We need a mute button”—something Jared’s fans have never asked for and likely never will.

You’ll also likely find her in bed reading a good book or endlessly scrolling TikTok (spoiler: it’s usually the latter) while listening to Harry Styles’ entire discography on repeat. Follow her on Insta to be inundated with pictures of her meals.

#6959. Amber Glenn Reveals She Was Drug Tested Ahead of ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Finale

“Third time they’ve tested me this season, by the way.”

Amber Glenn and Pasha Pashkov came in second during an absolutely incredible Dancing With the Stars finale, and their flawless freestyle is even more impressive knowing how little sleep Amber’s running on. The Olympian told Variety that she was drug tested the night before the finale, saying, “I actually had...you saw the drug testing show up last night. Yeah, so didn’t get much sleep. Third time they’ve tested me this season, by the way, guys, what’s happening? So, I’m excited to get some rest.”

At this point you’re probably wondering if Dancing With the Stars is drug testing contestants, which, nope! As Variety notes in their Twitter caption, Amber is referring to being tested by USADA (United States Anti-Doping Agency), which is the “national anti-doping organization (NADO) in the United States for Olympic, Paralympic, Pan American, and Parapan American sport.”

As their website puts it, they’re “charged with managing the anti-doping program, including in-competition and out-of-competition testing, results management processes, drug reference resources, and athlete education for all United States Olympic & Paralympic Committee (USOPC) recognized sport national governing bodies, their athletes, and events.”

Since Amber is currently training for the U.S. Figure Skating Championships (taking place in Utah this January), she’s being tested by the USADA like all athletes. Which might come as a surprise to everyone who didn’t realize Amber’s been juggling training with DWTS!

Her coach, Damon Allen, explained to KRDO “She's been balancing dance on a rigorous schedule with that, as well as, being able to continue training, getting ready for nationals in January. It's been tiring. But she knows how to recover, and she's got a regimen, and she knows how to take care of her body, and figure out how to be at the top of her game at all times, whether it's in the dance arena or back on the ice.”

#6944. Well, It Sure Seems Like Mitigating Climate Change Is No Longer a Concern for Democrats

Conservative Republicans and their oligarchical allies are getting everything they want out of a bipartisan bill that could walk back important environmental laws.

Last Thursday, and on a bipartisan basis, the Senate approved the so-called Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act (BAAJA), an alleged effort to “reform” the federal permitting process for things like energy projects, hard-rock mining, and, yes, data centers. At the very least, the bill lived up to one part of its clumsy title; its passage through the Senate truly was bipartisan. It passed by a 62–33 vote, and liberal Democratic senator Sheldon Whitehouse of Rhode Island was one of its strongest advocates.

It’s also a very big apple with more than a few razor blades concealed inside. Environmentalists have been giving BAAJA the side-eye for a number of reasons. Out in Nebraska, Democratic state chair Jane Fleming Kleeb suspects the bill is connected to a sudden revival of interest in restarting our old friend, the Keystone XL pipeline, the continent-spanning death funnel meant to bring the world’s dirtiest fossil fuel from Alberta ultimately to refineries around the world.

My problem with this whole thing is that this “kind of civil war” is taking place exclusively on the Left. Conservative Republicans, and their oligarchical allies, are forcing the action before the new Democratic majorities take over in January. They know exactly what they want out of the bill and what they don’t want in it. The president already has said there won’t be any wind farms under the provisions of this bill on his watch. (The birds! The whales!) The bill now is with the House of Representatives. May I suggest a discreet dragging of the feet? It is the holiday season, after all.

#6942. This $120 Dyson Airwrap Dupe Works Even Better on Fine, Thin Hair

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Admittedly, when the Dyson Airwrap came out, I wasn’t entirely convinced it was meant for my hair type. I watched all the reviews: People with long, luscious hair achieve, well, long, luscious hair. My bob haircut (and fine strands) could never get hairstyles like that. Then, I became a beauty editor with access to all the fancy beauty devices, and I finally tried it. Within weeks, it replaced all of my hair tools. I could smooth my ends, give myself a ‘90s blowout, add waves, dry my hair quickly—all with one device. And it’s significantly less damaging too.

But since it was released, a zillion Dyson Airwrap dupes have come out, offering up similar results for a fraction of the price. Because that’s the catch: The Airwrap is great, but it’s a whopping $650 ($500 if you get it on sale right now for Black Friday). My curiosity is piqued every time a new multi-styler launches, but my go-to favorite for the past few years is the Ella Bella 6-in-1 Professional Hot Air Styler. And while it’s 20% off for Black Friday (at the lowest price I’ve seen all year), I’m breaking down exactly why it’s my favorite for fine, short hair.

With six attachments, this all-in-one air styling tool was made to replace virtually every other device in your cabinets. It features a hair dryer, two rotating curling irons (one for each side of your head), and three hair dryer brushes (one oval, one round, and one paddle for straightening). Like the Dyson Airwrap, this device uses hot air, not heated plates, to style, which helps reduce heat damage. The airflow on this thing is pretty damn powerful, reaching 110,000 RPM. And there are three temperature modes and two speed levels to help you tailor your styling to your hair type and needs (typically, the thicker your hair, the higher the temperature should be; and the same goes for speed). The device also boasts a “noise-reduction design,” so it sounds a little quieter than others.

My hair is truly as baby-fine as it comes and dries naturally straight. For this reason, it’s usually very soft and silky (love), but refuses to hold a style for longer than an hour or so (hate). I also color my hair (nope, not a natural redhead), so it’s damaged and prone to breakage. I’m growing out my bob haircut right now, so it’s a little past my clavicle—à la the clavi cut—and I have bangs that always require heat styling to look good. Currently, my go-to styles are typically a smooth blowout that makes my ends look less stringy in the bob grow-out period, or soft waves that add movement and texture. All this to say: The Ella Bella Styler has a lot of work to do (sorry!).

I received an initial rendering of this tool nearly a year ago, and it quickly became my go-to Dyson Airwrap dupe. In fact, I keep it at my desk at the office to style my hair after morning workout classes. The brand just released this slightly upgraded version that I hadn’t used yet, and upon first impression, it looks significantly higher quality. When I opened the carrying case and felt the tool, it’s lightweight (aka easy to hold for a long time if you are working with much more hair than I am) but looks luxe. The packaging is

Totally. At under $150 (a true fraction of the cost of the original Airwrap), the Ella Bella 6-in-1 tool is a pretty solid option if you’re looking to spend less money but still get a high-quality tool. I especially love how it works on my short, fine hair, but I could see someone with long hair falling in love with this too. I’ve already stashed mine in the green room at the office for midday touch-ups, and two people have asked if they could try it.

#6937. What’s a Vintage Car Rally Really Like? Inside the Louis Vuitton Dolomites Classic Run.

How I got mixed up in a race with a bunch of priceless historic automobiles and views of Italy’s spectacular Alpine scenery—and won.

In vintage rally driving, there are generally two types of competitor. Both share a passion for keeping priceless and nearly century-old vehicles in use on the open road rather than displaying them under glass. But some see the drive itself as challenge enough, while stalwarts adhere to what’s called the road book. This is a hundred or so pages of complex navigational directions that the copilot uses to navigate instead of a map, maintaining a constant flow of instructions to the driver. It is this book and how closely you follow it that decides the eventual winner.

Gianandrea Redaelli, a car nut whose family has long roots in the Italian printing business, is firmly in the “road book” camp. In early September, I climbed into the copilot seat of his 1939 Aston Martin C-Type Speed Model Roadster Bertelli—just one of two ever built, with an experimental aerodynamic body that earned it the nickname “the Flying Pea”—and stayed there as he drove six hundred kilometers of “left at the traffic lights, right at the cow” for the Louis Vuitton Dolomites Classic Run. The rally started in Venice and ended, after a scenic but challenging detour high into the Dolomite mountains, at Monza, the home of Italian Formula 1, just in time for practice day of the Grand Prix.

Venice might not seem the most logical starting line for a car rally given the proverbial wetness of the streets. But the city was chosen this year because it was also the end point of the last LV rally of its kind: the La Serenissima Run from Monte Carlo to Venice in 2012. Gianandrea won that one as copilot, with his father, Giuseppe, at the wheel. The pair also won the one before it—the Boheme across Hungary, the Czech Republic, and Austria—in 2006. This time, however, the pressure was on me too.

I have been a copilot before, and I can tell you it’s no picnic. Rally racing in vintage cars is less about speed and more about timing. It’s an exercise that requires pinpoint accuracy at the time-stamp points in the overall course and, most critically, in the regularities. These are fiendishly short time-over-distance sections in which crossing the line just a few hundredths of a second late—or early—earns strict penalties. Sensibly, the day before the race began, Gianandrea took me out in the Aston in the Veneto countryside to practice, especially those critical regularities.

I could have opted for an easy life. The rally allowed competing cars, dating from 1931 to 1969, to use the road book or follow the course using a map in a dedicated phone app. But where’s the fun in that?

“If you join a competition, you follow the regulations and strive to do your best,” Gianandrea says. “As an Italian, if you paint numbers on the door of my car, I will want to win.”

Every car in a rally like this comes with its own idiosyncrasies. On flat ground, someone driving a souped-up 2026 sports car would find these tests difficult to nail. In the reality of race conditions, the regularities often involve steep climbs or descents and unexpected corners, alling for an expert hand at the wheel—and foot on the gas pedal. The tendency of some car doors to swing open at hard bends (ours has the “suicide” doors with hinges on the back instead of the front, beloved by early carmakers) only adds to the excitement. The purpose of driver and copilot is to battle and overcome all the mechanical and computational challenges—and get our ride to the finish line bang on time.

Louis Vuitton has been involved in these glamorous and exacting vintage rallies since 1993. The Dolomites Run represents an enthusiastic return to the genre after a fourteen-year gap and, perhaps tellingly, the company’s global sponsorship (since 2025) of Formula 1. Unconfirmed rumors circulated in the field that the choice of ending the rally at a Grand Prix could well be repeated—and soon—in other countries in the future.

It’s easy, after all, to see why a fusion of style and cars with six- and seven-figure price tags might appeal to Louis Vuitton and competitors alike. Not surprisingly, at every coffee and lunch stop, the LV effect sprinkled the race with an added sense of luxury. In between, our snaking caravan carried the glamour of a century of motoring along with it. At every hairpin you got a glimpse of a gull-wing Mercedes, a 1931 Bugatti two-seater (my personal fave), and a shimmering behemoth of a Bentley in quick succession. These cars would turn heads anywhere. But in Italy, arguably the home of the sports car, they brought smiles, applause, and gasps in even the tiniest village that passed by in our slipstream. I swear I even saw a nun with a glint in her eye at one set of traffic lights.

Navigating, I was quickly learning, is not so much about providing a constant babble of information as it is knowing how to give clear and simple instructions—and only when strictly needed. As the finish neared in Monza at the end of two long days on the road, I was able to give Gianandrea the most helpful instruction of all: “Nothing for 15 kilometers.”

“That,” he very kindly replied, “is the mark of a pro.”

Even after the engines cooled, we were still clueless about how well we’d done overall. That changed at the final event, a dinner inside the Sforzesco Castle in Milan. Gianandrea stepped up, beaming, as winner of the race, and for the first time as driver. I stood somewhat sheepishly behind him as copilot as he received the trophy from Pietro Beccari, CEO of both Louis Vuitton and Louis Vuitton Fashion Group. It was an impressive swirl of Murano glass created by Venini, housed in its own equally impressive traveling case, made (naturellement) at Louis Vuitton’s atelier in Asnières.

Gianandrea has promised me visiting rights.

#6936. Burgundy Cat-Eye Nails Are the Most Mesmerizing Mani of the Season

There are a few nail colors I consider non-negotiable on my fingertips once fall rolls around: chocolate brown, black cherry, and, of course, burgundy. The latter is a rich, wine-like shade that’s classic, sophisticated, and something I find myself coming back to year after year. For 2026, the color has gotten a seriously mesmerizing makeover in the form of burgundy cat-eye nails. It’s not only a shimmery, dimensional manicure that’ll have you staring at your fingertips all day long, but it’s also a certified trend with searches for the look up more than 300 percent on Google. “Burgundy cat-eye nails have been one of my favorite looks to create behind the nail station,” says celebrity nail artist Erica De Los Santos.

Unlike a traditional glossy burgundy manicure, cat-eye nails use magnetic polish to create a light-catching effect that shifts and moves depending on the angle. Basically, it scratches a really specific itch in your brain that you’ll definitely become obsessed with (if you aren’t already). With the help of De Los Santos, I have all the deets on how to get burgundy cat-eye nails, plus plenty of photo inspo.

Burgundy cat-eye nails are exactly what they sound like: a moody and sophisticated rich, wine-red manicure created with polish that contains magnetic particles. This delivers a shifting shimmer that looks like velvet or polished gemstones—just enough sparkle to make these perfect for the holiday season.

“What makes this look so beautiful is how the depth and color shift change depending on the undertone of the base and the magnetic shade layered over it,” says De Los Santos. “The result is a multidimensional finish that catches the light beautifully, giving the nails a luxurious, almost velvet-like appearance.”

And the best part? There are so many ways to make the look your own. The magnetic effect can be customized depending on how much shimmer you want, and you can work a cat-eye into all types of nail art. “One of my favorite ways to wear burgundy cat-eye nails is as a solid color because the magnetic effect is already so beautiful on its own,” says De Los Santos. “But if you want to have a little fun with it, you can pair it with chrome starburst accents, playful polka dots, or even animal prints. The beauty of a burgundy cat-eye is how versatile it is.”

If you tell your nail artist you want a burgundy cat-eye manicure, they’ll have no problem picking the perfect deep red shade. However, you’ll need to specify whether you want a soft velvet cat-eye finish or a more defined cat-eye look. Bringing a reference photo is always helpful (gotcha covered below), since the placement and intensity of the shimmer can completely change the finished look.

After prep (“I always start by gently pushing back the cuticles, carefully removing excess dead cuticle, and shaping the nails”), De Los Santos applies one coat of a deep plum or burgundy gel polish and lets it cure.

Next, “I apply one coat of magnetic cat-eye gel, preferably in a shade similar to the base color, such as deep red, plum, or purple,” says De Los Santos. “Keeping the shades within the same color family really enhances the depth and makes the magnetic effect pop. Before curing, I use a magnet to create the desired light-reflecting effect.” She flash cures (a short, incomplete cure that’s a few seconds long) each nail as she applies the polish and magnet, and then fully cures all of them at the same time once every nail is painted. From there, leave your cat eye as-is or add chrome powder, designs, or any other nail art you love.

You can also create the look at home with magnetic nail polish and a compatible nail magnet (talk about an easy DIY nail art moment!). De Los Santos explains that gel gives you more time to manipulate the magnetic particles, but she also says there are lots of traditional polish options out there to get the cat-eye effect. With regular polish, prep is exactly the same, but the only thing that’s different is the application. “Apply a coat, immediately use your magnet while the polish is still wet, and move on to the next nail,” says De Los Santos (working with the polish while it’s still wet is key to get the magnetic particles to sit where you’d like them to). “Once you’ve completed all ten nails, you can repeat the process with a second coat if you’d like to build more depth and dimension.” Finish with the appropriate top coat.

Personally, I can’t think of a better manicure for the holiday party season. Check out some inspo pics below.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmo. She’s covered and tested all things nails, from the coolest new polish colors to cuticle care and nail art trends.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmopolitan and was previously the beauty assistant at Good Housekeeping, Woman's Day, and Prevention.

#6933. Does Anyone Care How Yellowjackets Ends Anymore?

The survival drama is finally back for its last season. But finding out the answers to the show’s mysteries isn’t all that exciting anymore.

Believe it or not, Yellowjackets is based on real events. In 1972, a high school rugby team, along with their families, boarded Uruguayan Air Force Flight 571 and crashed in the Andes. Only sixteen of the forty-five passengers survived when they were rescued seventy-two days later, and they all harbored a dark secret: To survive, they ate the flesh of the people who had died.

Yellowjackets, in a way, explores what might have happened had the group never revealed just how they managed to survive in the wilderness. Much like Uruguayan Air Force Flight 571, the Showtime series follows a high school women’s soccer team that survives a plane crash by killing and eating each other. Cocreators Ashley Lyle and Bart Nickerson blended in potential supernatural elements such as a dark presence that possibly forces them to kill, classic Lord of the Flies homages, and a killer soundtrack. Yellowjackets also cut ahead to each survivor’s life as an adult, featuring the acting talents of Melanie Lynskey, Christina Ricci, and Juliette Lewis. For a while, it was engrossing TV.

The series explored survival in all its forms, from the lasting effects of grief to the need for women to escape their mundane, suburban lives for a little danger. It was critically acclaimed, nominated for awards, and topped many “Best of the Year” lists. But then, five years and two more chaotic seasons went by that really seemed to test its audience’s … well, appetites.

Now that the fourth and final season is upon us—season 4 premiered on Friday, November 20—the answers to Yellowjackets’ burning questions will finally be revealed. Namely: Is something forcing them to kill each other, or can everything be explained by science? Will the public find out that they resorted to ritualistic murder and cannibalism? And, most importantly, is there anyone left who’s still interested in finding out?

To be fair, there’s already something of an answer to that last question. Despite critical fatigue for the series, over two million viewers tuned in to the season 3 premiere last year, per Paramount. That number then increased a bit to 3 million by the season 3 finale. Sure, those numbers pale in comparison to The Pitt season 2’s 15.4 million average weekly viewers or even the Landman season 2 premiere’s 9.2 million viewers. But there is a fan base willing to see this series to the end, whatever happens.

What is that ending? Where the series currently stands, our girls are finally returning home after being rescued. They’ll likely adjust to normal life again by committing another hunt back in their hometown. And in the present, the promise of “last one standing” in the pre-roll recap likely points to a final bloodbath. Is that even what fans want? Until that finale, how everything will resolve, and what it all really means, remains a mystery.

For the Uruguayans, they faced a moral and religious crisis when they returned. When news broke that the rugby team ate the flesh of their dead passengers, one of the survivors defended their actions by comparing it to that of Jesus Christ and the Eucharist. According to the survivor, the group made a pact to sacrifice their bodies for each other if they perished so that they could eat their flesh to survive. The media circus completely flipped, and they were heroes once again. Pope Paul VI even wrote them a letter to sanctify their actions. It’s a fascinating end to their story—but one that the Yellowjackets cocreators likely found made for an entirely different sort of TV show.

Outside of the pagan imagery and supernatural threats that the Yellowjackets cast face, religion has yet to enter the picture. The cannibalism mostly seemed inevitable until it finally happened halfway through season 2, and besides that, the women have done much worse since returning home. After three seasons of one cold-blooded murder after another, the body count just to keep their secret hidden is enough for all of them to face the electric chair. And even if there is a malevolent force controlling them, it may take a metaphorical release of pain and grief on par with Hereditary or Weapons to fully satisfy viewers in the series finale.

“To some extent, I do feel as though if we were to just 100 percent jump both feet on one side of [the supernatural/reality] line, it would disperse some of what makes this show interesting,” cocreator Ashley Lyle told CBR ahead of season 4.

“I think that we are fully aware that we are either going to delight or anger both sides of that equation in some way, shape, or form,” they added. “But I don’t know that that could have been avoided.”

At this point, some version of watching the women all kill each other, or even watching them all defeat the evil they believe is haunting them, just doesn’t feel like enough. The show’s biggest problem might have just been that it ran too long. A simpler ending to a shorter story might have left viewers delighted. But we shall see! If Yellowjackets can land the plane (sorry) in the end, I’m willing to eat (apologies again) my own words on this one.

#6931. Enhance Cozy Vibes With These 8 Captivating Woody Perfumes

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Spritzing these picks feels like cuddling up in your favorite sweater.

If you thought woody perfumes were relegated to your noncommittal Hinge dates, I’m about to introduce you to a whole new world of woodsy scents that are captivating, soft, and totally addicting. I promise these are far from the strong, heady, and cloying woods that have emanated off of the men in your life.

You see, woody notes are extremely versatile. Depending on what you pair them with, they can bend into so many fragrance families. Woods add body to really ground a scent, hence why you'll typically find them at the base. But they can also make for a soft opening too. It's really all about the full composition.

Which brings me to the best woody perfumes. Don't think it's all about cedar and sandalwood perfumes, though (granted, many of our favorites contain these, of course). There are so many unique takes on woody perfumes, whether you want to evoke a cozy fireside vibe or gravitate toward earthy scents filled with herbs and spices. Cosmo editors test perfumes for a living, and we've gone ahead and compiled the absolute best woody perfumes. From soft versions for woodsy newbies to the boldest and deepest scents for extra-cold weather, everything here is vetted and truly loved by our team. Lemme show ya some of our top faves:

You’ll be a woody perfume fanatic after smelling these eight gorgeous scents.

One Cosmo editor says: “I’m not exaggerating when I say this is my most-complimented perfume of all time. (And I own over 600 perfumes, FYI.) This is sexy and spellbinding, and though it features vanilla, it’s the woody notes that make 21:50 Reverie so captivating. Soft smokey tobacco gets a juicy burst from plum and a touch of sweetness from vanilla before this turns deep and sensual with cedarwood. I’ve worn this on plenty of dates, and other men have come up to tell me how incredible I smell.”—MARY HONKUS, CONTRIBUTING BEAUTY EDITOR

One Cosmo editor says: “I’ve never been to the Scottish Highlands, but I feel like I’ve been plopped directly into the rainy area when I spritz this hauntingly beautiful perfume. It somehow captures that feeling of looming fog with iris butter and musky amyris, then wraps me in the comforting and spicy woody warmth of a forest.”—MH

One Cosmo editor says: “This has slowly become one of my favorite scents of all time. Hints of almond and incense help it strike the perfect balance between airy sweetness and grounding woods. Like all Liis fragrances, it feels effortlessly natural on skin. Bonus: The packaging is so beautiful. I love displaying it on my countertop.”—JESSICA NEISES, SENIOR FASHION EDITOR

One Cosmo editor says: "I first sniffed Maison Louis Marie Bois de Balincourt in the form of a candle years ago, and ever since it’s been one of my favorite scents ever. It’s woody and earthy, which usually isn’t my vibe, but it’s got some sweetness and spice that makes it smell more fresh and universal. Whether I’m dousing this on as a perfume or burning it in my home, the creamy fragrance is just so comforting."—JASMINE HYMAN, ASSISTANT BEAUTY EDITOR

One Cosmo editor says: "This is my all-time favorite woodsy perfume—hands down. It smells like camping. Not the smokey campfire or the sticky marshmallows, though. But the woods in the fire, the dew-covered tree trunks in the early mornings, and the cozy smell of my musky sleeping bag."—BETH GILLETTE, BEAUTY EDITOR

One Cosmo editor says: "Contrary to what the name suggests, this winter perfume doesn’t smell like smokey firewood. Instead, it perfectly replicates (lol, pun unintended) cozying up by a fireplace with a cup of hot cocoa, which is honestly the most quintessential wintertime activity. The notes of roasted chestnuts and clove oil are intertwined with a hint of wood and cashmere to give off a warm, comfy sweater vibe. But don't worry, there's still a tinge of smokiness that gives campfire aroma, just in a gentler way, thanks to sweet vanilla. Obviously, I also have the candle, because I'm obsessed."—MH

#6927. Dakota Johnson Wore Cherry Cola Nails, So Now I’m Wearing Cherry Cola Nails

A syrupy upgrade to your typical burgundy mani.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

One of my guilty pleasures is sipping on a large vanilla cherry Coke at the movie theater. I’m really not a big soda drinker, but there’s just something about the combination of a fizzy, cherry-flavored soda, a bucket of popcorn, and a good movie that does it for me. And luckily for me, I’ve recently discovered another way to indulge: cherry cola nails. Yep, my go-to concession stand order is officially making its way to my fingertips, and naturally, I’m eating (or should I say drinking?) it up.

Dakota Johnson was also spotted wearing cherry cola nails earlier this month at a screening of Flesh Impact in Los Angeles, which only further fueled my need to get the look. Similar to syrup nails, these have a glossy finish to them, but are dark, rich, and reddish-brown, just like the classic soda. It’s basically the perfect marriage of two of my favorite fall nail colors, burgundy and chocolate brown. Ahead, everything you need to know about the trend, plus plenty of inspiration for your next nail appointment. Movie theater soda optional... but highly encouraged.

Think of a Cherry Coke: It’s dark brown with touches of red when the light hits just right. And that’s exactly the goal of the manicure version. “Cherry cola nails are the perfect blend of deep burgundy, rich chocolate brown, and a hint of red,” says celebrity nail artist Jacqueline Pham. “Think of that beautiful, dark cherry soda color that looks almost brown in certain lighting but has a cherry red undertone when the light hits.”

They’re also a great way to dress up your nails for the holiday season: “It’s a little sultry, and a fun alternative to your classic burgundy red manicure,” adds Pham. “You can play around with different shades of brown and burgundy and a slight change in either color can create a completely different cherry cola effect.”

Need some ideas of how exactly to incorporate the color onto your fingertips? Firstly, Pham suggests wearing them as a single-color mani—glossy and straightforward with a classic, high shine finish. “It’s timeless, elegant, and looks beautiful on both short and long nails,” she says. She also likes adding a little shimmer too, which you can do with the help of Essie’s Gel Couture Liquid Diamonds in Size Me Up or Color Street’s Nail Strips in Velvet Dahlia. If you’re craving some nail art, you could also do a twist on a classic French manicure with cherry cola tips over a sheer nude base.

First things first, you’ll want to reach for more than one bottle of nail polish. “The secret to achieving the perfect cherry cola shade is layering your colors rather than using just one,” says Pham. “I personally recommend starting with a rich chocolate brown as your first coat, followed by a deep burgundy as your second coat.” This mimics a cherry soda too, with a brown base and a hint of cherry flavor and color. “The brown creates a warm base, while the burgundy adds cherry tint,” explains Pham. After applying a base coat, she says to do “one thin coat of chocolate brown and one thin coat of burgundy, allowing each layer to dry.” After those two coats, she says to “finish with a high gloss top coat to give it that glassy, soda-like shine.”

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmo. She’s covered and tested all things nails, from the coolest new polish colors to cuticle care and nail art trends.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmopolitan and was previously the beauty assistant at Good Housekeeping, Woman's Day, and Prevention.

#6924. Artist Quentin Jones Launches a Surrealist Jewelry Collection

With Nocturne, the House of Quentin Jones founder brings the playful, sculptural language of her homewares to a collection of wearable art.

Above: Artist Quentin Jones wears pieces from her new jewelry collection while posing in front of tapestries Circe’s Garden and Western Moon.

British mixed-media artist Quentin Jones has quite the varied resume—one that spans artistic disciplines and collaborators as varied as Chanel, Apple, and Miley Cyrus. Now based in Brooklyn, her practice blends filmmaking, photography, collage, and painting.

Last year, Jones turned her eye towards interiors, launching a homeware line and design studio called House of Quentin Jones. The brand debuted with a collection of tapestries and ceramic sculptures that are both architectural and playful, leaning into Jones’s moody, dreamy look. Now, after a year of HOQJ, Jones is releasing new styles—and adding jewelry to the mix.

Nocturne, a collection of sculptural jewelry, translates Jones’s eclectic design language into wearable art. She pulled motifs like eyes, hands, and celestial forms from her tapestries and ceramics and incorporated them into the pieces. The collection features earrings, pendants, chains, and cuffs that balance black and white, pearl and onyx, organic and sculptural shapes.

“I’m interested in jewelry as sculpture,” Jones tells ELLE Decor. “A cuff wraps around an arm, a collar frames the neck, a tassel moves. The body becomes part of the composition. I want the pieces to have a presence of their own, while leaving room for the personality of the person wearing them.”

As she enters this new category, Jones is also beefing up her existing offerings. She’s adding new quilted tapestries to the studio’s repertoire and iterating on previous designs. With these new pieces, she says, “The palette has become softer and earthier—ivory, sand, and burgundy—but the compositions still have that surreal, graphic quality that runs through my work.”

But she isn’t stopping there. Jones has big dreams for the future of her eponymous studio, a “House” built larger with each new release. Next up, she’d like to expand the accessories category further, hoping to add bags, scarves, and other wearable pieces to her lineup.

“I’ve spent years working between art, film, and fashion,” she says. “I’m building towards a collection of things that can accompany you through your life—from what hangs on your wall to what you choose to wear.”

#6923. Couples Need to Stop Being So Weird About GTA 6

Grand Theft Auto VI came out last Thursday, and somehow the most unbearable thing about it isn't the grown men playing the game—it's the couples.

After the release of the game, my feed became a nonstop slideshow of printed contracts, handmade gift baskets, and girlfriends narrating their boyfriends' disappearance into Vice City like it's a deployment. Couples posted printed agreements that grant the player 10 full days of uninterrupted access.

I'm not here to tell anyone not to play a video game. People should do what makes them happy. But why are we all being so weird about a video game?

Ten days off from the relationship, so a man can play a game. I'd like to meet the woman who has ever been granted a 10-day leave from her partner, her family, her job, and the emotional labor of every relationship in her life. Women don't get to just disappear to pursue their hobbies.

Some women in relationships are putting together “offerings” for their partner. One viral example involved a GTA-themed gift basket stocked with snacks and drinks for the player's sessions. Another woman posted a Target-run basket of her boyfriend's favorite snacks with the caption, "This is your sign to make your man a GTA 6 basket!"

No. It's not. When was the last time he made you a basket for one of your hobbies?

On the flip side, some men are gifting their partners a basket, with the idea that women need something in return—a little distraction to keep busy while he's busy. Women are not toddlers who need to be handed a snack and a coloring book while the grown-ups play. We have friends. We have jobs. We have entire lives that existed before his Rockstar account and will exist after it. We'll be fine. We might even enjoy the quiet!

It all begs the question: Why can't men just have hobbies like normal people? Women have hobbies all the time. We go to pilates, we read, we get absorbed in a show, and nobody drafts a legal document. Let’s keep up that energy. Just play the game and also make time for the rest of your life!

But it isn’t just about people finding the time to play the game. There’s separate discourse among couples about the relationship players maintain with Lucia, the main female character.

Online, some women have expressed feeling jealous of Lucia. Around the game's lead-up, users on TikTok and Instagram were claiming that boyfriends who nurture a virtual relationship with Lucia could be considered cheating. A YouGov poll of 4,000 adults found around 6 percent would consider dating in games like GTA VI to be infidelity. That's a generally small number, but it's still making headway on social media.

Lucia is a character. She is pixels. If your relationship can be undone by a woman who exists only to rob a gas station with your boyfriend, the problem was never Lucia.

To be fair, some women have leaned into the joke. One girlfriend gifted her boyfriend the game with a four-rule counter-contract that ended with the line "Lucia is not your real girlfriend." I respect the bit—but it still icks me out.

On the other hand, there have been people claiming that GTA 6 is teaching men to be better partners. I’d like to politely disagree. The theory goes that watching Jason treat Lucia well, and putting effort into that relationship on screen, will make players better boyfriends in real life.

I'm glad they're being attentive to Lucia. I mean that. It’s a nice change to see the video game encouraging men to be decent human beings (though the bar is low). But at the end of the day, they're buying flowers, fixing things up, and doing the emotional work for a woman who doesn't exist, while their actual girlfriends are across the room eating a basket of Swedish Fish.

Can we all get real for a second? What grown man needs a video game to teach him how to treat a partner? Showing up, communicating, and putting in effort are not secret skills only unlocked by a video game. I promise you your boyfriend is not learning valuable relationship skills playing GTA 6.

So to any couples out there being particularly weird about GTA 6: play the game, enjoy it, and please just be normal. Be a person with a hobby. It really isn't that deep.

#6920. Why Do We All Love a Small-Town Holiday Romance? Guarding Stars Might Have the Answer

The fantasy of falling in love in a small town has surprisingly little to do with the small town itself.

I’ll find any excuse to cozy up with a holiday rom-com, so when Guarding Stars dropped on Netflix, it was a no-brainer. Gossip Girl and Supernatural alumni Leighton Meester and Jared Padalecki are embracing one of my favorite tropes: the good old “small-town holiday romance.” Leighton’s character Hannah goes with Jared’s character Jack to his hometown pretending to be his girlfriend, while actually serving as his bodyguard. It got me thinking: Whether we’re die-hard city dwellers or seasoned travelers, why do we still love the home-for-the-holidays romance trope?

For me, the answer lies in how this specific genre taps into our own deep-rooted desire for escapism. We’ve seen these kinds of fake girlfriend or friends-to-lovers storylines play out a million times before, so their predictability is incredibly comforting to watch. We have, quite literally, seen this film before. And during the heightened chaos of the holiday season, it places viewers in the safe bubble many people crave.

Plus, the small-town setting in Guarding Stars is perfectly cozy. The two head to Jack’s obnoxiously large family home in Montana that’s been pristinely decorated in Nancy Meyers–inspired aesthetics. And much like in one of my favorite holiday movies, The Family Stone, the house itself is just part of the fantasy. The idea of being welcomed into a close-knit community with acceptance might be exactly what we dream of the holidays being like. Being loved by a family that, in the case of Hannah, is not even your own is really intoxicating.

Even the dating looks more fun. Hannah and Jack spend the whole movie dating their way around town, doing cutesy romantic things like attending the local rodeo, getting caught on a kiss cam, and mucking out horses together in the family stables. These wouldn’t be your go-to date locations in a more urban, city-based setting, so their dating experience feels playful and different from everyday life.

And Guarding Stars finds a way to elevate this trope. Hannah isn’t actively looking for a man when all of this unfolds. She’s just doing her job as a bodyguard when she inadvertently falls into a snowball of festive shenanigans. Hannah is doing fine on her own, but there’s something sweet about watching her let her guard down. Guarding Stars gently suggests that by Hannah making herself a little more vulnerable, she’s making room for more people to love and support her. As someone who tends to handle the hard stuff alone (eldest daughter syndrome, anyone?), that really resonated with me.

So often, this genre leans toward women going to the small town and being “fixed” or changed by the relationship they encounter. Their lives in the big city are unsatisfying, actually, and they really would rather work at the Christmas tree farm. That doesn’t happen here. She ends up back in New York City, and that’s where the final action set piece of the movie happens.

Guarding Stars shows that the small-town holiday romance trope can change and evolve without losing its festive magic, because it’s about comfort and connection. We can be cozy and keep our apartments in New York City, and I’m thankful for that this holiday season.

#6917. Most Black Friday Sales Are Fake. These Ones Aren’t.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The Internet is a deep, dark place this week, but we found where the deals are worth it.

How do you even define a good deal anymore? The whole point of Black Friday was for retailers to make us wake up at the crack of dawn the day after Thanksgiving, wait in line, and then fight someone over a TV that was 50-percent off at Sears or Best Buy. How did they do that? They would sell a few products (door busters) at a loss just to physically bring people into the store. Those deals were good, and everything else, which you would buy because you were already there, was where the money was made.

That is now a thing of the past. Lines and physical altercations aside, in the past 20 years there has been a real swing in the favor of big retailers. First, we created Cyber Monday (though most people do their Black Friday shopping online already). Then the deals started a week earlier. Now, they last all month. And instead of having door busters to get you in the door, retailers just have very aggressive online marketing programs. In fact, it turns out that most Black Friday deals aren’t even deals at all. In a recent study, it was found that one in three Amazon Black Friday deals provided no true savings at all—prices were marked up earlier in the year, only to be brought back down to a standard retail value. The big retailers like Amazon and Walmart are the most to blame for these tricky strategies, but you should still be judicious when looking at any so-called Black Friday sales.

The good news? Black Friday is still filled with great deals—you just have to know where to look and what to expect. A lot of big wins revolve around private sales at more niche, direct-to-consumer stores. For example, Filson, one of our favorite outerwear brands, is having a 25-percent-off sale for Black Friday. That’s not the 75-percent-off you might have wanted, and there are other times that Filson goes 25-percent off. But the best strategy these days is to think about holiday shopping: Just buy the things you already would, but do it while they’re on sale now.

You should be especially skeptical of tech products. The increase in all tech prices has made it easy for those brands to push a 10-percent-off sale when products where 10-percent-off the current price. This is the sort of stuff we’ve been watching as the sales roll around. Based on this and years of covering sales and knowing what’s good and what’s not, we determined these are the best deals of the weekend. These are the deals our editors think are actually worth shopping this Black Friday.

Mr. Porter has an absolute goldmine of a sale. Like sure, you can grab this Brunello Cucinelli Jacket for $6,500 at 50-percent off. But it’s also filled with basics from brands like On that rarely go on sale.

These Nike socks are more fun than the pairs they sell on amazon. And they’re $6. Also better than Amazon.

$644 for a cashmere-blend overcoat that’s normally $1,298 is a real-life steal.

A few days ago, the Samsung Frame Pro was 50-percent off. That deal was real, and it sold out. Today the Hisense Art TV (which we’re fans of) is 50-percent off. These deals vary daily, but today, this is the one we’d buy.

Dyson deals are confusing as fuck. You need to be knowledgeable about the model you’re buying into, and here that number matters. You will be able to find models like the V8 and the V10 for super cheap this week—but that’s because those models are old and outdated. Right now, the best and newest Dyson cordless vacuum is the V16—with all the bells and whistles it retails at $1,100, but it’s $200 off right now.

But as someone who has used every Dyson, I know the V12 is a damn good model. And while it isn’t the newest, it still has lots of the same features as newer models and will last you years. So the fact that it’s on sale for $400 this weekend makes it a truly good deal. Also, I can’t find this deal on Amazon. For the best deals from Dyson, I would always check its actual site.

As previously mentioned, we love that many of Filson’s jackets are 25-percent off this week. This is a good example of when a brand just wants to participate in a sale, so they’re willing to mark down popular items. This jacket is so good our commerce editor Luke Guillory endorsed it last week, calling it the best $250 jacket. Guess what? Now it’s only $187. We know for a fact that this deal won’t last past Black Friday.

It’s expensive, yes, but Begg x Co. is one of the best cashmere brands anywhere in the world. $500 off a new cashmere sweater is something that doesn’t happen until the end of the season. But if you buy it now, you still have six good months of wear to get in.

Water filtration has become very expensive, especially when it comes to reverse osmosis and a countertop version that looks sleek. We have been recommending this one for over a year at $500, so the fact that it’s $150-off for once makes it a pretty good deal.

Furniture on sale is a whole category we can’t ignore. Furniture markups are a real thing, and a lot of it has to do with quality and materials. Marble is overpriced for sure, but good quality marble is worth it. We love Soho Home’s marble, and this dining table has been one of its most popular pieces for a while. Getting it for almost $3k-off when you’re not a member is a big deal.

If you’re on the home redo kick, then a brand like Le Maé, which is offering some its best wood pieces at 50-percent off, is a great place to start. Made with high quality materials and oak, this is one of those furniture pieces you will own forever.

If you’re a camo guy or do any non-deal hunting, then you know that Tom Beckbe jackets never go on sale—but this is your lucky week.

#6915. A Case for Being Single During the Holidays

valentine’s Day is the official holiday for couples—so why does the fall and winter holiday season feel like a love and relationship fest?

This time of year has literally been deemed “cuffing season”: You’re encouraged to take couples pictures at a pumpkin patch, bring a date to Thanksgiving, drink hot cocoa by the fireplace at Christmas, and have someone special to kiss at midnight on New Year’s Eve.

But even though we’ve been inundated with countless signs that this is the best time to couple up, I’m going to go out on a limb here and say that I actually think there’s no better time than the holiday season to be single.

“The holidays can actually be a great time for creating our own traditions and sense of self,” relationship expert Bethany Nicole tells Cosmopolitan. “Being single over the holidays gives us a much-needed time of rest, reflection, and recalibration.”

So instead of falling victim to the pressures of coupling up during the holidays, why not just lean into being single instead? You might even find yourself enjoying the holidays more. Why? Thought you’d never ask:

According to Newsweek, 65 percent of Americans admitted that they’re stressed about the amount of money they’ll spend during the holidays. And for 73 percent of them, this financial stress actually makes the holidays less enjoyable. Can you guess who people are spending most of this money on? That’s right: their children and their romantic partners.

Sure, you might still have to get your parents, siblings, and your weird aunt a holiday gift—but isn’t it satisfying knowing that you don’t have to budget a huge chunk of change for yet another person in your life? By staying single during the holidays, you’re actually keeping your pockets happier.

The holidays are a time to spend with loved ones. But with so many events centered around the holidays, you might be spending more of that precious time with someone you’re just getting to know instead of with your friends and family. Assuming you like your friends and family, this is not the best ratio!

When you’re single, you can spend the holiday season with whoever you want. You can go to endless friendsgivings without having to drag along a plus-one, you can eat Thanksgiving dinner comfortably with your family without having to check on someone else, and it’s actually really fun to go out on New Year’s Eve with your best friends.

On the other side of the coin, family time in general is not for the weak. I get that, because mine isn’t either. If you were in a relationship, you’d probably be panicking right now about how to explain to your partner that your aunt is really funny if you ignore that she hates your mother or that your dad salutes the turkey before he cuts into it. But when you don’t have a date to Thanksgiving, you can just enjoy the weird and disturbing quirks of your family without having to explain them away to your partner.

When you’re coupled up, you’re expected to be “selfless” and “generous.” Ew. When you’re single, on the other hand, you can just be your normal, selfish self—and I truly think that’s beautiful.

Someone with a partner would probably be expected to give up the last piece of the pumpkin pie or be giving with an extra big piece of turkey. But because you aren’t in a relationship, you can have as much pie as you want—without pretending like you’re not picking up the speed while eating so your partner doesn’t get more bites than you.

Being around your own family for the holidays is torture enough—imagine if you then had to add a partner’s family to the mix? Not just that, but you’d most likely have to pretend to like them, too. And even get to know them!

Meeting an S.O.’s friends and family is a lot of pressure. Even though the holidays are a time of joy and celebration, they’re also super stressful. And you just don’t need the added stress of trying to impress your boyfriend’s dad, who doesn’t believe women should be in leadership positions.

Sure, having a partner around the holidays is nice—doing romantic things with someone you care deeply about can really add to the experience. But if you really want a partner, you have the rest of the new year to try and find someone.

For now, I say save your money, hang out with your friends, and do what the holidays were intended for: eating pie until you’re sick.

#6914. This New Jaeger-LeCoultre Watch Looks Perfect for Robert Pattinson’s James Bond

The actor’s favorite watchmaker has unveiled a pink-gold Reverso with impeccable timing.

Just a week after Robert Pattinson was announced as the next James Bond, Jaeger-LeCoultre, the Swiss watchmaker for which he's an ambassador, has unveiled a new Reverso that looks rather well suited to 007.

It's certainly a good match for Pattinson's personal tastes. “I definitely like really thin watches,” he said in a recent interview. “That's the main thing. In general, I would probably prefer a leather strap.”

The new Reverso Classic Monoface Small Seconds checks both boxes. Measuring just 7.56mm thick, it has an 18-carat pink-gold case, a silver-colored dial with a texture inspired by stacked coins, and a black alligator strap. Its smaller proportions–40.1mm by 24.4mm—recall the original Reverso of 1931, whose ingenious swiveling case was designed to protect the watch during polo matches. Inside is a manually wound movement with a 42-hour power reserve.

At $18,400, it's a somewhat more elegant alternative to the more beefy dive watches traditionally associated with Bond. And with its art deco styling and slim profile, it would slip neatly beneath a dinner-jacket cuff with an ease the next Madeleine Swann would surely appreciate.

Of course, Omega has been Bond's official watch partner since 1995, and there's no suggestion that's changing. But as a watch for the man playing Bond, this new Reverso feels intriguingly on point.

#6910. Beauty Sets to Gift Every Kind of Main Character on Your List

Tap into their current hyperfixation with one of SHEGLAM’s holiday collections.

When you’re holiday shopping for your nearest and dearest, you might find yourself racking your brain for gifts that (a) appeal to their niche interests and (b) they don’t already own. After all, everyone has their thing, whether it’s an encyclopedic knowledge of a TV series, a record-breaking number of blushes, an insatiable sweet tooth, or a series of manifestation rituals they swear by. And, if you know where to look, that thing makes the perfect jumping-off point for their gifts.

This holiday season, SHEGLAM is stocked with beauty gift sets that will appeal to the distinct personalities in your circle, from the Twihards and Potterheads to the nostalgic beauty lover and more. Keep reading to find the perfect match.

The Potterhead in your life likely already has the books, the DVD box set, and maybe even their own Golden Snitch perched on their desk. What they don’t have, however, is this Potter-themed beauty vault. Inside, they’ll find a Marauder’s Map-inspired eyeshadow palette, a mystery lip oil topped with the Sorting Hat, blush, eyeliner, setting mist, primer, and a DIY lip gloss kit they can mix up like a custom potion.

Highlight drops that sparkle like the skin of a forever teen, blood-red lip tints, an eyeshadow palette as dark and moody as the weather in Forks, Washington—Twihards will be dying to get their hands on this set. While we can’t time-travel back to the era of OG beauty influencers, we can bring back some of the makeup trends that defined the 2010s, like bold, matte lips. Gift a throwback-beauty enthusiast a three-piece set of liquid lipsticks with vibrant color payoff and a matte finish that feels blissfully velvety and comfortable on the lips all day (unlike the formulas we swiped on back in 2016).

#6900. This London Townhouse Was Designed for the Holidays

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Miles Redd and David Kaihoi created the ultimate English Christmas retreat for longtime clients—cozy, festive, and swathed in reds and greens.

Above: In the living room, the seating is by Luther Quintana with Samuel & Sons fringe. The wallcovering is by Iksel, and the cocktail table has a Fornasetti top.

Some homes are for summers by the water, others for weekends on the slopes. When Krystyna Houser set about redecorating her family’s London townhouse, she was thinking of Christmas. “We spend every Christmas there,” she says. At this house, that meant more than just putting up a tree.

Located near Richmond Park, in southwestern London, the 19th-century townhouse has been in Houser’s family for more than 20 years. It was always a place to return to while Houser, founder of the nutritional startup mBIOTA Elemental, and her husband Dan, a co-founder of Rockstar Games, were raising their three children in New York City. Dan had grown up nearby, and the ample greenspace that gives the area its name made it an appealing place to visit with a young family. The interiors, however, had never quite received their due.

Houser turned to Miles Redd and David Kaihoi, the partners behind the New York–based firm Redd Kaihoi. She had known Redd since 2012, when she hired him to decorate a Brooklyn Heights townhouse, and the two became friends. They have since worked on multiple houses together. For London she trusted the designers to craft the warm and celebratory atmosphere she craved. “I wanted it to be equally elegant, cozy, and family-oriented,” she says.

Red and green were a natural starting point, as green already had the family’s allegiance. “It’s one of their favorite colors,” Redd says. “So we leaned into that a lot.” Verdant hues appear in velvet, felt, and marble throughout, while the entrance hall is dressed in crimson. These were holiday colors with no intention of coming down in January, and Redd was going for sophistication rather than holiday kitsch. “I wanted romantic 19th-century elegance, the feeling that they’re stepping into a Merchant Ivory movie.”

This approach suits a firm known for exuberant color and layered furnishings. Redd brings a sense of theater to traditional decorating, drawing on the work of such legendary designers as Billy Baldwin, Albert Hadley, and John Fowler and mixing lacquer, chintz, and bold geometric patterns. Before opening his practice in 1998, he trained with antiques dealer John Rosselli and decorator Bunny Williams. Kaihoi, a sculptor, joined the ELLE Decor A-List firm in 2008 and became a partner in 2019. Together the duo give grand rooms an ease and wit that keep formality in check.

Houser considers “interior designer” an insufficient description for Redd and Kaihoi. “I feel like they are set curators,” she says. She gave them considerable freedom, occasionally inviting them to surprise her with potential purchases. “I don’t think it’s my place to hinder creative expression, and, as a result, the rooms are just magical.”

The house’s existing architecture gave that freedom some useful structure. “It had great English bones,” Redd says. He retained the original living room mantelpiece, library doors, and front door, while upstairs he and Kaihoi worked to restore character lost to earlier alterations that had stripped away 19th-century details. An excavation created additional basement space for a screening room and a ping-pong area, giving the family more places to gather and play.

From first visit to completion, the project took the better part of an unhurried decade, with pauses for permits, the Covid pandemic, and other commitments along the way. All the while Redd and Kaihoi kept shopping at auctions, antiques stores, and fairs, which ensured that the rooms would have an accumulated character. The designers looked to the guards at Buckingham Palace for the entrance hall and main staircase, borrowing the red and gold of their uniforms for its matka silk walls trimmed with gold braiding. Beyond the hall, the green velvet–lined library opens into a living room papered to resemble a coromandel screen.

Upstairs, the primary bedroom and a sitting area occupy a long, narrow space. Green felt screen dividers give the bed its own area in one corner without a wall breaking up the suite. “It’s all sort of one big bedsit, which is a very English notion,” Redd says. A small painting of a parrot on one of the screens keeps Houser company at her bedside.

The couple wanted their suite to feel like a luxurious hotel—inviting places to read and work, a comfortable bed, and a generously scaled bathroom—without sacrificing a sense of home. “You’ve ruined hotels for us,” Redd recalls Houser telling him of their previous work.

Step Inside the Ultimate Christmas Retreat

But Houser’s favorite rooms are the ones that bring everyone together: the kitchen and the adjoining striped family room, which open onto the garden. It’s here where everyone gathers for meals and to relax. The connected spaces provide separate places to eat, play games, and watch television, allowing the family to spend time together even when everyone is doing something different.

“Their design helps bring us together,” Houser says. “And that’s what it is. It’s a family home.”

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

#6899. Bono and Anton Corbijn Open Up About the Creative Partnership That Changed Rock ’n’ Roll History

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

It is the most extensive collaboration ever between a band and a visual artist. Here, the frontman and photographer take Esquire behind the scenes of the album covers, music videos, and photos shoots that have defined their careers.

“Bono is usually a man with a lot of ideas,” says photographer/director Anton Corbijn, “so initially we have a conversation and then maybe I make it more theatrical or whatever.”

That overly casual-sounding process has led to the most extensive collaboration ever between a band and a visual artist. Since their first encounter in 1982, Corbijn has helped establish the evolving public image of U2 across album covers, music videos, and photo shoots for almost 45 years, including the landmark cover of The Joshua Tree, the all-out aesthetic reboot for Achtung Baby, and music videos including “Pride (In the Name of Love)” and “One.” (The partnership is extensively documented in Corbijn’s 2005 book U2&i.) Most recently, they reconnected for a connected pair of surreal and evocative videos—“The Greatest Show on Earth” and “Can’t Stop Us Now”—from U2’s new album Carnaval de Luz, the band’s first full-length release in nine years.

Bono recalls first noticing Corbijn’s work when he was still a teenager, especially a photo of Joy Division that ran on the cover of NME in 1980 following the suicide of lead singer Ian Curtis. “Seeing Anton make these images of these people, they were actual human beings,” he says. “We think from their music that they’re certainly not, but then these images prove that they’re people who lived just across the way. It’s like making movies.”

Indeed, Corbijn has gone on to direct films, including the acclaimed 2007 Ian Curtis biopic Control, as well as doing videos and album covers for Nirvana, Tom Waits, Metallica, the Rolling Stones, and R.E.M. and decades of ongoing work with Depeche Mode.

“It’s difficult for a photographer to shoot bands,” he says, “because bands have an identity, and you need to somehow get that right. It’s very important that the pictures I do with Depeche Mode don’t look like the pictures I do with U2.”

Recently, Bono (in New York City) and Corbijn (in Amsterdam) met up on Zoom to discuss their unprecedented alliance, playfully referring to each other as “A” and “B” and recalling shared moments and mutual inspiration. (They also revealed that Corbijn made a film about the American leg of the 2017 Joshua Tree 30th-anniversary tour, which they are planning to bring to local screens during next year’s Carnaval de Luz: The Greatest Show on Earth dates.)

“There are, of course, many occasions where a great artist springs up on their own—a painter, a songwriter, a photographer, a filmmaker, novelist,” says Bono. “But often there’s some kind of community. U2’s music certainly comes out of community, and Anton is a huge part of our community.”

This conversation was edited for length and clarity.

Bono: The great stories in songwriting are often unexplained, not resolved. And Anton, your gift to U2 was that you want the emotions to cohere rather than to explain the music. If everything adds up, it adds up to nothing. It’s the thing that you can’t explain that keeps you going back to the work—which sounds like that might be an excuse for laziness, for half-finished lyrics, but I felt over our time together that you finished some of the lyrics for me with your images.

The comedy and the tragedy of “The Greatest Show on Earth” is a real example of that. I hope it’s not too close to the bone with the singer playing the leader of a religious cult and the women doing all the work. But look, anyone who can get the members of U2 to put on beards and look like ZZ Top is some sort of shaman. To do one of your film clips is to risk being very silly—and whilst I don’t have that fear in general, as is obvious from the pratfalls I’ve made in my life and artistic life, the other three members of the band do have a certain amount of self-respect, and so only their faith in you as an artist and trust in you as a friend would have them participate at the level they did.

Anton Corbijn: I don’t want to make anybody look silly, but weird is good, and strange, and like you said. I have a very intuitive approach, and we collaborate. Famously, what became the cover of The Joshua Tree was me looking at the desert for a photograph for an album U2 were calling Desert Songs.

Bono: Yeah, yeah, that’s right. Another [potential] title was The Two Americas. But wasn’t it on that shoot we found the title? Via you, and especially the way you pronounced “Yah-shua Tree.” I hope you still find it amusing that we find your Dutch accent amusing.

But as often happens in art, the reason becomes clear years later. That’s the strangest thing—it didn’t start with the Joshua tree; it’s at the end you discovered it. On Carnaval de Luz, we knew from the beginning what we were thinking about, but we discovered it at the very end with the image maker, with the icon maker.

Corbijn: I remember we had a three-day shoot, and we were driving from Reno to Palm Springs, and after the first day, we walked together to the hotel in the desert. And I told you about this tree that I liked so much. I had this landscape camera where the lens moved, so you could get very long shots. I thought it was really nice that you have a tree on the front, and the image goes on to the back, and the band is on the back. So I was talking about that, and I mentioned the name of that tree. And then, the next morning, you came down with the Bible in your hand and said, “It’s got to be The Joshua Tree!”

Bono: We had no idea that Joshua is the same name as Jesus, Yeshua, and what post-punk band would put an album out called The Jesus Tree? But we forget, because the world is going through some parched times presently, that even though the ’80s seemed to be great for a lot of people—you had the Material Girl and dance music, and New York was flying—there was something up in the ’80s. Springsteen would say there was a darkness on the edge of town. And I think for us to dress as pilgrims, as Irish people coming to America, was absurd but actually very effective.

We were trying to figure out, How do you not look stupid on a magazine cover? How are you to be? I mean, we were in our early 20s, and we were not elegantly wasted, as was kind of the mood of the time—if you’re in a rock ’n’ roll band, you should have the good manners to at least look like you’re about to die any minute. You don’t want to look earnest. And we needed to find our own way of, How do we be ourselves? What masks can we choose to reveal ourselves? What is the iconography of whatever the project is? That naturally led on to Zoo TV and all the various shoots we did for Achtung Baby and Zooropa and all that.

My favorite session of ours, Anton, is when we shot for Pop in Miami. They’re some of my favorite photographs of the band because you seem to photograph what the work is rather than what the people are. So for Pop, you went for bright colors and mischief and charm. Anton, do you remember those sessions in Miami?

Corbijn: Yeah, vividly actually, because you were still recording at the time, in the basement in a hotel. We were young and playful, and so many different options with clothing as well. And we tried funny-looking shots and playful shots, serious stuff as well, but I think after The Joshua Tree, I was attempting to bring more life into the pictures with color use. I also remember you saying that at the time that you liked the colorful stuff I did with Depeche. It was not new for me, but we did cross-processing so the color became very vivid, and that’s why Achtung Baby looks so colorful.

Bono: We always live by this sort of modus that the songs will tell us what to do. The songs will tell us how to look, how to behave, what the live show is about, how to dress onstage and sometimes even offstage—and because your thing was, “I’m shooting the song; I’m shooting the material, not you,” I think that really worked for us.

One thing I’d like to ask you, Anton: Would you be comfortable talking about the religiosity of your work? It’s a recurring theme for you, and can you explain a little bit of that?

Corbijn: Not really, actually. I don’t go to church anymore. My father was a pastor, and my grandfather, and all my uncles, and so it sort of made me walk away from it. But I think because I grew up with it, it’s just in my visual language. We grew up next to a cemetery, and maybe that had something to do with it, but the visual iconography, the Christian iconography, is in our society so much that it’s hard to not use it. Sometimes it’s just there—like the first pictures I did with Depeche Mode in ’81, when they were a teeny-tiny pop band, was in this studio in London, and there was this cross with Jesus on it in the garden. So in the photograph, they were like teenybop guys, but still Christ is hanging in the picture.

Bono: Religion has often been such a negative force in the world. It’s very understandable that we would try to avoid the obvious imagery associated with it, but in my life it’s turned into a very positive thing. It’s sort of buoyed me up. But you have it in all your work, despite your attempt to get away from it, I suppose.

Corbijn: I was just thinking, in the “One” video, there’s the Father, Son, and the Holy Ghost, and the band, and the woman. It was all in there. I think it’s very nice that I knew your father and you met my father.

Bono: Yes, that’s right. Two powerful men in our lives. But again, your ability to talk people into doing things—you asked my father to stand on a seesaw, and he went along with it, he was fascinated by it. And then you got the four fathers of U2, the fathers and sons.

Corbijn: That was one of my favorite pictures of U2. There’s a lot of depth in it.

Bono: What other favorites do you have, Anton?

Corbijn: Well, I made a book of 500 pages on U2, full of pictures I like. I like some of the photographs we did around Rattle and Hum. That was not a good time for me, but I think there’s some good pictures there. Also on the rooftop in L.A.—I don’t mean the playing one, but the one when we looked at the rooftop, when we discovered it.

Bono: I found this hotel, a real hotel called the Rosslyn Million Dollar Hotel. I was reading Bukowski at the time, listening to Tom Waits, and I walked into this hotel. I couldn’t believe it existed. Eventually I managed to convince Wim Wenders to make a film there. And we so loved the sign, the Million Dollar Hotel, that when we played on that rooftop, we rebuilt the sign.

Corbijn: Yeah but on a much smaller rooftop. I’m in the video, actually. I’m trying to get through the crowd, pointing to the policeman that I have a pass, and he goes, “You go that way!”

Bono: That poor man, that policeman who pulled the plug. By all accounts, his children tortured him, and he was the most unpopular cop in Los Angeles. He was just doing his job.

Let me see if I one more question for my hero and friend...
